wanted to kind of discuss the actual AI maturity curve and what this looks like for SDRs. So
starting out, when I was using AI, I was just prompting back and back and forth. And I wanted to
kind of dive into the actual AI maturity curve and what this looks like for SDRs. So starting out,
when I was using AI, I was just prompting back and forth with a chatbot. I'd do typical things.
Like, I'd ask, can you help me actually draft this email or kind of perfect this copy or do this
account research for this specific account I'm looking at. And what we've seen kind of progress over
the last year is first the idea of co-pilots. And so co-pilots are AI that can actually go out and
carry out a true task. So instead of talking back and forth, getting email copy and then copying
that into your Gmail, it's something now that we see like actually having the AI go and basically
hook up to a Gmail MCP or an API and be able to actually send that email off for you. And really
with bot, what we believe is kind of the next frontier is actually automating entire jobs in full.
And so going beyond just a task and actually doing an end-to-end workflow. And really with the staff
function of actually combining a team of bots, you can see how kind of automating a job, but also
combining that with different features of business like talking to RevOps or talking to your AE and
getting context on an account. And how bots can actually pull in that context for you and really
help you do that full process. And so just diving into what Grockbot actually looks like if you
haven't seen it before. So you have all your bots kind of here on the left-hand side and they're
doing different things. Like we have a sales outbound bot here, we have our chief of staff, and you
can message them just like you message your own teammates. And more interestingly, you can actually
see them talk with each other. And I have my own Grockbot instance set up where really there's only
one bot that I'm actually talking to. And so for the most part, I've kind of offloaded all that
context to my chief of staff actually talking with other bots and doing that all for me. And I think
some important things also to emphasize here is that bots keep their own context at a per bot level
and memory, so they can really improve as you work more with them. And as you go, their memory gets
better. And this is why I really emphasize you actually should be training your bots as well,
depending on the task with kind of the relevant info. So set up your Slack, set up your Gmail
connector, and you can actually kind of start to train it on what your voice looks like. And so
specifically for Gmail, what I do is I've had it go into my Gmail and actually look at how do I
actually talk to people. And specifically in my outbound messages, how do I actually talk to people
in a way where they actually want to respond, and they're interested in kind of seeing a demo or
something forward. And so I'll really go into more of what that actually looks like, how I place
weights on certain things to actually help Grockbot understand this. But I think a big thing I want
to talk about also is the ability to share your bots. And I'll dive into this more in the next
slide. But I think as SDRs, one of the most important things is being able to see what your
teammates are actually doing and to kind of easily transfer those skills over to your other SDRs. So
you can actually really scale those outputs and do it very quickly. I think the truth, at least at
SpaceX, of what we've seen is that what's working for us today was not working three months ago. And
three months before that, it was something entirely different. And so the ability to really just
move quickly and always be iterating is something that the ability to share bots is really going to
help you with and help scale the entire SDR team. So diving into this more, like why are we using
Grockbot? I think to start, it's as easy and as intuitive as just iMessage or another general
platform. You can just talk to your bots. And I think more importantly, bots are always on. And so
you don't have to walk around with your laptop open anymore. You can actually close it. I can go on
coffee walks and continue prompting from my phone. Or I can set it up on a long running task and
then trust that it will actually complete that task end to end and do the thing without me having to
kind of object in between and guide it. It really is the confidence to go carry out and do things.
And there's many ways you can actually improve that by recording your own screen and some other
stuff that I'll show more in the demo. But again, it also uses the tools just like you do. And so
you can think of your basic connectors of MCPs, of APIs, everything that you've already kind of set
up. But beyond that, what's really important is it actually owns its own VM. And so you can log into
the browser. And for the parts of your SDR work that are a little more difficult or there's no real
way to automate them, you can actually show bot in the browser. You can sign in to those tools. And
you can actually show bot by recording your screen, recording you go through it. You can show the
exact workflows that you do in there. The really tedious and specific stuff that's kind of hard to
explain or maybe there's no real like open API for it. And so this is really something that I found
immense value in. And what's given me the confidence to be able to say, okay, grok bot, you can
genuinely go end to end with what you're doing here. And as I talked about before, I think shareable
templates are one of the most important things. And I think every single team at a team level should
basically all be spending time building their own templates, sharing those with the team, getting an
understanding of, okay, what are the things that are actually working? And how can I not only just
import that template, but how can I import it and then add my own nuance onto that of whatever like
my tonality and my messaging is, or actually maybe it's just I like to do my workflow a slightly
different way, but actually kind of taking this as a base template and then working beyond that. And
you can also prompt grok bot directly and ask for direct improvements. So you can import templates
and be like, hey, look at this template my colleague shared with me. What do you think are things
with my own workflow that you think would be a good addition to build on top of this or to kind of
get more value out of that. And so I really emphasize just actually like talking back and forth with
your grok bots and kind of like starting to really push it to help you. And I think this is why we
emphasize so heavily that grok bot is really just a multiplier for your own work and really a way to
scale the outputs you're doing. And as an SDR, I don't think there's anything more important than

I think one of the most important things is going end to end. And this is something I hope I showed
in my demo, but that I'm really trying to push. Is that it's very simple to just like prompt back
and forth with GrokBot. Or to have GrokBot do a single task. Like sending an email for you. But I
think really you should push it to do the entire workflow. And it's a lot of pain up front for you
to prompt back and forth. To build this system. To figure out like what actually makes this system
work. But if you stick through it and you're persistent. And you train it on your style. You will

prospecting. It's able to enrich them. It's able to understand all the context. And then it's able
to push that out and actually outbound them. And do the full process. And I think that's really what
differentiates GrokBot. And what makes it a true like force multiplier for SDRs. So I really
emphasize like thinking at a high level. And kind of thinking how you can actually build that
system. And I think on that same note. You should be intentional here. So it's really intuitive to
just prompt each bot. And what I highly recommend is just taking a step back. And thinking, okay,
let me build this all through my actual chief of staff. Bots should be able to talk to each other.
They should have that context. The point of this is to offload more work from you. And help you
actually get higher outputs. And so you shouldn't actually be focusing even harder. And messaging
even more bots. It's exhausting from just all the prompt and context switching you're doing there.
And lastly, what I kind of hope I really showed my demo. Is how to think systematically about your
setup. I think it is very easy to just always want to make new bots. It's kind of fun. And it's kind
of exciting. But as someone who's built an army of bots. I promise you I've had way too many at some
points. And it's honestly more chaotic. And it does more harm than good. And so you should really be
questioning like. Why is it important that a new bot does this? Why can my existing email bot. Not
just write my emails. And also be able to be my inbox manager. Like why do I need a second bot for
that? And also on that note like when should I just turn things into routines. That that bot runs.
But really I emphasize kind of thinking about all these different things. As ways for you to
actually just save more tokens in the first place. But actually just also not confuse yourself. And
not kind of just get overloaded with bots that are just doing like kind of similar things. And again
making these through your chief of staff. Makes it all the more easier to actually do that. All

depends on kind of the amount of maybe gone calls you're pulling from. The amount of context you

freshly enrich. Versus how many are kind of old context that you're pulling in from Salesforce that
you already have context on. So I think it very much depends. It also depends at the scale you're
running this on. But I think the best way to kind of do this is honestly like prompt back and forth
with GrokBot. Get an understanding of where your token usage is at. And you can also talk back and
forth with GrokBot to find ways to optimize your workflow. So maybe 50 daily prospects isn't
something practical. Depending on like the scale of outbound you're doing. Like maybe it makes more
sense to actually run 250 on a weekly basis. And that just sends on a Monday morning. And then you
have that to work through the whole week. I think mine might be just a little more daily because I
like pulling in all that context and really like pushing GrokBot as hard as I can. And I think I'm
just using a little more tokens doing that. But yeah, I highly recommend just trying it out on that

stage and we'll be back right after that. Nice. Hey, everyone. Thank you for joining today's
workshop on Grokbot for customer support. My name is David and I am a software engineer here at
SpaceX AI in the user ops org. I'm really excited to share what we've been working on and how we're
using Grokbot for customer support. So here's the agenda for today. I'm going to quickly give some
context on Grokbot in case you haven't been watching the live stream or been to one of the other
sessions. Then I'll share a few specific use cases for it around customer support. Then I'll demo a
lot of these. My goal is that the session is a little bit more demo heavy so you can really see it
in action. And finally, we'll finish with some Q&A. So let's get started. So first, what is Grokbot?
Grokbot is a new product that lets you hand off real work to an AI coworker. The interface looks
very familiar, just like a messenger app on your phone or a computer. But under the hood, it's built
to keep working after you've sent your original message. So you aren't just chatting back and forth
with quick, simple one-offs here and there. You're really able to delegate a real project to Grokbot
and it can go off and take it from there. One of my favorite things about Grokbot is how flexible it
is to the way you work. So you can start with a single general bot to help you get set up quickly
and easily. Or create a team of specialists that coordinate around the task and work in parallel on
certain subtasks. And however your team operates, Grokbot can fit in and be very useful very
quickly. So why Grokbot? Why does it feel like interacting with a capable coworker rather than just
another AI tool? For me, there are three big reasons. First, Grokbot is always on. So by now you may
have heard that Grokbots have their own computer. All that really means is that they can work
without using yours, which is a little bit more powerful than you would think. So if you close your
laptop or you are working on something else, your Grokbots can still finish the task you assigned
them without you having to check in constantly and steer them too much or slowing down your local
machine. There are also routines, which are cron jobs or event-based triggers that you can launch
your Grokbots with and make them super reactive. This way you don't have to be online at the exact
moment you want them to run or start working on the task. And these are super simple to set up. All
you have to do is tell it, create a routine at this time or on this event, just like a normal
conversation. And it will set it up for you. Second, it is really easy to use. The interface is
familiar for a reason. You don't have to be an engineer that is familiar with the ins and outs of an
IDE to use bots effectively. You can just talk with it normally like you would a friend or teammate
on Slack. And finally, it fits how you actually work. There are already connectors for most tools
you use. You just have to go into the marketplace and search for them. And there is likely one for
you. So you can plug Grokbot immediately into your ticketing system, whether that's Plane, Zendesk,
Intercom, where your team chats like Slack, where your knowledge base is like Notion. And then from
there, you can use the bots and routines like building blocks that fit the way your team actually
works. And one really powerful thing about Grokbot is if there is a missing piece, you can likely
have Grokbot build it. Grokbot has access to cloud agents. So if there is something that you're
missing, you can just have it tell it to spin up a cloud agent to build a certain connector or a
certain thing, and it can go off and do it for you. So you can get creative and build around, and
you're not stuck waiting on Eng Roadmap or a third-party vendor to add support for the thing you
need. Cool. Now we'll get into some customer support use cases. So the big one is always, can it
answer support tickets? The answer is yes. And I'll show this later in the demo. I know this can be
scary at first to let an agent sort of reply to your customers. So you can definitely build up to
it. So you can crawl, then walk, then run. At first, you can have it just read tickets. Maybe give
you a summary and the root issue of what the user is asking about. And maybe it can draft a
response. Then you can have it add the draft to a note in your ticketing system on the ticket. So
you can sort of see it before you press send. And finally, when you're confident, then you can have
an answer and actually respond to the ticket and the user. There's also things you can do around the
guardrails and environment you give GrothBot. So you can set up evals and traces fairly easily so
you know what it's thinking and what actions it will take before it publishes. So when you can add
traces to those draft steps and then sort of make sure it's behaving the way you want it to before
going on. Cool. The second one. So at scale, you may have a lot of support tickets with a ton of
different customer states. And it can be really challenging to find the things you should care about
in that exact moment that need your attention. GrothBot can make this a lot easier through alerting.
So you can spin up a bot that looks for certain things. For example, maybe your team is hyper
focused on churn this quarter. And you want to know when someone writes in that is threatening to
churn that has been a customer for six months or more. Anyone on your team can spin up a GrothBot
that you have connected to your ticketing system. You can just tell it to create a routine that
looks through your tickets. Every hour classifies if a user is threatening to churn and has been a
customer for a certain period of time. And then send you an alert in the Slack channel so everyone
has visibility. Yeah. And another one that maybe is more powerful than you think or is harder to get
to than you think is an internal question and answer agent. So if you've used other support agents,
they are primarily focused on customer replies. It's surprisingly cumbersome to leverage the
knowledge knowledge base you've already sort of created or accumulated to answer your own teammates
questions. You may have someone in GTM that is trying to get up to speed on today's release about
cloud agents. So they can come prepared to a customer call or a TSE manager looking for changes to
the internal refund policy over time and if that has impacted certain customers. You have already
done the hard work of creating this knowledge base of both public info so think your docs and help
center as well as likely some internal info so you might have suggested operating procedures around
how your teammates handle refunds. So that is not public information. You should be able to query
this intelligently within Slack and GrokBot makes this really easy. And maybe the last one and one
of the cooler ones is it can improve itself. So finally, you can give GrokBot your traces or have it
look back on the last week of tickets and sort of ask it to look and try to find improvements for
tickets that maybe could have been handled better, could have been flagged earlier, and it can go
and do those things for you. Cool. So next we're going to get into the demo. I have created this
demo. I have a team of four bots. They are build, so it sort of runs the setup and the
infrastructure reply. So this actually answers users in both our ticketing system and then Slack.
There is an alert. So this one usually gets pinged by reply and post an alert in Slack and tags me.
And then tune, which tries to improve the system as we go. I want to call out that you definitely do
not have to have these in mind or set them up before getting started. In fact, I don't recommend
that. Instead, you can start with one bot, name it a generic name, and try to teach it one workflow.
Then when the scope grows or you need more than one running at a time, I recommend breaking them up
naturally then and reorganizing them in a way that makes sense to you. Cool. So now I'll get into

you can and should feel comfortable setting guardrails for your bots. You can see, saw maybe at the
end there it was even more protective than I wanted it to be, but that's probably better for writes.
Have them be read-only to start and then work up to writes and have manual approvals for certain
actions. You can also scope permissions to your different bots. So one bot might have permission to
post automatically to Slack, one might not and make them approve those actions before they do them.
Second, start simple. So the whole product is still so new. I don't think there's one meta or one
clear playbook. And the product is meant to be very flexible. So try to make the bots fit into how
you work and not vice versa. Third, try to get a little creative and experiment. You can really have
it build what is missing. Cool. Thank you so much. That is the rest of the presentation. And we can

is you have like a Postgres database. Tell it to like create a table for eval runs or for a set of
evals. So things you want to test every single time you like make a change. And then another table
for traces. So every time reply runs, I ask it how long it took, what files it looked at, what files
it ended up choosing to reply. So usually it's like a filtered down list. And then from there you
can usually decipher where it went wrong or things like that. And that's something you set up in the
reply bot configuration? Or is that like a meta, you know, bot that's looking at the whole system?

up a connector with -- to the build bot with Supabase. Told that it has access to Postgres now.

new eval. Or run a trace or answer a ticket even if it's internal, external, even if it's a dry run
ticket. So even if it's just leaving a note on the plain ticket, still writing a trace. So then I
